The [Northrop X-21A][ref_x21] is the only aircraft in this series that worked and was cancelled for it. Two [Douglas WB-66D][ref_b66] bombers were given new wings cut with thousands of fine spanwise slots, and pumps inside the wing drew a thin sheet of air through those slots to hold the boundary layer laminar. **It did what it was built to do.** By the end of the programme the aeroplane was flying with laminar flow over more than ninety-five percent of the area intended for it, in [Braslow 1999][book_braslow_1999]. Then the programme stopped. This article is the twenty-second in the [X-Planes series][related_post_a297_xplanes_framing], following the [X-1][related_post_a298_bell_x1], the [X-2][related_post_a299_bell_x2], the [X-3][related_post_a300_douglas_x3], the [X-4][related_post_a301_northrop_x4], the [X-5][related_post_a302_bell_x5], the [X-6][related_post_a303_convair_x6], the [X-7][related_post_a304_lockheed_x7], the [X-8][related_post_a305_aerojet_x8], the [X-9][related_post_a306_bell_x9], the [X-10][related_post_a307_north_american_x10], the [X-11][related_post_a308_convair_x11], the [X-12][related_post_a309_convair_x12], the [X-13][related_post_a310_ryan_x13], the [X-14][related_post_a311_bell_x14], the [X-15][related_post_a312_north_american_x15], the [X-16][related_post_a313_bell_x16], the [X-17][related_post_a314_lockheed_x17], the [X-18][related_post_a315_hiller_x18], the [X-19][related_post_a316_curtiss_wright_x19], and the [X-20][related_post_a317_boeing_x20].

**The received account is that laminar flow control failed on maintenance.** The slots clogged with debris, insects and rain, and the maintenance the intricate suction system demanded ended the programme. That account is widely repeated, and the [Northrop X-21A][ref_x21] reference entry gives it. This article is largely about one problem with it. **The service-maintenance evaluation was never performed.** It was one of the programme's stated objectives, the programme had not yet been able to address it when the money ran out, and the advisory group that reviewed the programme in October 1965 recommended a major wing modification before any meaningful maintenance data could be gathered. The modification was never made, in [Braslow 1999][book_braslow_1999].

So the X-21 is remembered for answering a question it never asked. The standard inventory entry is [Jenkins Landis and Miller 2003 American X-Vehicles, An Inventory X-1 to X-50][book_jenkins_landis_miller_2003] and the vehicle compilation is [Miller 2001 The X-Planes, X-1 to X-45][book_miller_2001]. The indispensable source here is [Braslow 1999 A History of Suction-Type Laminar-Flow Control with Emphasis on Flight Research][book_braslow_1999], written by the engineer from the National Aeronautics and Space Administration, or NASA, who served as technical consultant to the Air Force on this programme, and the contemporary engineering account is [Kosin 1965][research_kosin_1965] in the Journal of Aircraft. Braslow reproduces the 1965 review group's report in full as a document appended to the monograph, the Northrop analysis of the cloud problem survives as [Hall 1964][ref_hall_1964], the description of the wing written while it was flying is [Butz 1963][ref_butz_1963] in Air Force Magazine, and the technical results of the Northrop final report are summarised, with their numbers, in [Joslin 1998b][ref_joslin_1998].

## The Research Question

About half the fuel a subsonic transport burns in cruise goes to overcoming skin friction, and on an aircraft of that size the boundary layer is turbulent over nearly all of it, in [Butz 1963][ref_butz_1963]. A laminar boundary layer exerts roughly a tenth of the shear. **The prize is therefore enormous and has been obvious since the 1930s**, and the history of the subject is a history of trying to collect it, in [Freeman 1935][research_freeman_1935], [Quinn 1944][research_quinn_1944], [Von Doenhoff 1944][research_von_doenhoff_1944], [Cliett 1952][research_cliett_1952], [Torda 1952][research_torda_1952], [Northrop Aircraft INC Hawthorne CA 1956][research_northrop_aircraft_inc_hawthorne_ca_1956], [Raspet 1957][research_raspet_1957], [Northrop Corp Hawthorne Ca Norair Div 1964b][research_northrop_corp_hawthorne_ca_norair_div_1964], [Northrop Corp Hawthorne Ca Norair Div 1964a][research_northrop_corp_hawthorne_ca_norair_div_1964_2], [Kosin 1965][research_kosin_1965], [Anderson et al. 1969][research_anderson_1969], [Khusnutdinova 1972][research_khusnutdinova_1972].

Laminar flow is not stable at these Reynolds numbers. It becomes turbulent through the growth of small disturbances, and suction through the surface thins the boundary layer and makes the profile fuller, which damps those disturbances. **That those disturbances exist at all, and grow at the rate theory predicted, was settled by [Schubauer and Skramstad 1947][research_schubauer_skramstad_1947]**, whose measurement of laminar boundary-layer oscillations turned boundary-layer stability from a calculation into an observed phenomenon and made everything the X-21 attempted arguable in advance. The theory and measurement of that mechanism are in [Trilling 1950][research_trilling_1950], [Ringleb 1952][research_ringleb_1952], [Schwartzberg and Braslow 1952][research_schwartzberg_braslow_1952], [Gortler 1957][research_gortler_1957], [Hasimoto 1957][research_hasimoto_1957], [Gribben 1959][research_gribben_1959], [Dennon 1962][research_dennon_1962], [Görtler's New Series Method 1962][research_gortler_s_new_1962], [Morduchow 1963][research_morduchow_1963], [Pate and Deitering 1963a][research_pate_deitering_1963_2], [Pechau 1963][research_pechau_1963], [Kozlov 1965][research_kozlov_1965], [Kozlov 1966][research_kozlov_1966], [Messiha 1966][research_messiha_1966], [Mills 1966][research_mills_1966], [Singleton 1967][research_singleton_1967], [Zamir and Young 1967][research_zamir_young_1967], [Kozlov 1968][research_kozlov_1968], [Leitsina and Pavlyukevich 1968][research_leitsina_pavlyukevich_1968], [Tokhunts 1968][research_tokhunts_1968], [Kozlov and Tsyganyuk 1969][research_kozlov_tsyganyuk_1969], [Baldwin 1970][research_baldwin_1970], [Gershbein 1970][research_gershbein_1970], [Pushkareva 1970][research_pushkareva_1970], [Kobayashi 1972][research_kobayashi_1972], [Kobayashi 1974][research_kobayashi_1974], [Savoshchik and Tokhunts 1974][research_savoshchik_tokhunts_1974], [Harvey 1975][research_harvey_1975], [Carter 1977][research_carter_1977], [Srokowski 1978][research_srokowski_1978], [Thiede 1978][research_thiede_1978], [Mack 1980][research_mack_1980].

**The period record behind that sentence is large, and it supplies about an eighth of this article's dated research records.** The 1930s to the 1960s produced a continuous literature on boundary-layer control, low-drag aerofoils, profile drag, transition and the behaviour of swept wings, gathered in the clusters below on suction laminar flow control, on natural laminar flow and low-drag aerofoils and on boundary-layer stability and transition, and represented here by [Von Doenhoff 1941][research_von_doenhoff_1941], [Holt 1943][research_holt_1943], [Zalovcik 1945][research_zalovcik_1945], [McCullough and Gault 1947][research_mccullough_gault_1947], [Brennecke 1948][research_brennecke_1948], [Loftin and Burrows 1949][research_loftin_burrows_1949], [Pfenninger 1949][research_pfenninger_1949], [Black 1952][research_black_1952], [Banner et al. 1958][research_banner_1958], [Gregory 1960][research_gregory_1960]. The programme was built on top of this body of work, and read together it makes the X-21 look less like an experiment and more like an engineering demonstration of a settled result.

**The X-21 was not built to discover whether this works.** It was known to work, in [Braslow 1999][book_braslow_1999]. It was built to find out three practical things. Whether a slotted suction wing can be manufactured to the required tolerance at acceptable cost. Whether laminar flow can be held on a large swept wing in real air. And whether such a wing can be operated and maintained by a squadron rather than by a laboratory, in [Joslin 1998b][ref_joslin_1998].

It answered the first two. It never got to the third.

## Programme Origin

Werner Pfenninger brought continuous-suction laminar flow work to the Norair division of Northrop after the Second World War, and through the 1950s that group ran an extended series of Air Force contracts on slot arrangements, crossflow stabilisation and wing design, in [Butz 1963][ref_butz_1963] and [Braslow 1999][book_braslow_1999]. The wind tunnel and glove experiments that preceded the aeroplane are in [Brennecke 1948][research_brennecke_1948], [McCullough and Gault 1948][research_mccullough_gault_1948], [Loftin and Burrows 1949][research_loftin_burrows_1949], [Pfenninger 1949][research_pfenninger_1949], [Northrop Aircraft INC Hawthorne CA 1956][research_northrop_aircraft_inc_hawthorne_ca_1956], [Lachmann 1959][research_lachmann_1959], [Gregory 1961][research_gregory_1961], [Groth 1961][research_groth_1961], [Pfenninger and Bacon 1961][research_pfenninger_bacon_1961], [Pfenninger and Groth 1961][research_pfenninger_groth_1961].

**The direct ancestor is a glove on a fighter.** In the middle 1950s Northrop flew a slotted suction section built into one wing of a Lockheed F-94, and the flights showed laminar flow over the whole of the suction section, in [Butz 1963][ref_butz_1963]. [Joslin 1998b][ref_joslin_1998] records that the F-94A glove carried 69 slots, that it held fully laminar flow in level flight, climbs, turns and descents, and that it lost laminar flow in cloud and regained it within 30 seconds of leaving the cloud, the X-21's cloud result seen a decade early on a smaller wing.

**The programme was approved in stages.** The review group's report, reproduced in [Braslow 1999][book_braslow_1999], dates the X-21A Laminar Flow Control Demonstration Program from August 1960, following a report of the Aerospace Vehicles Panel of the Scientific Advisory Board on boundary layer control dated 25 June 1959, and lists the reviews that followed it. The Vehicles Panel reexamined the programme in November 1961 and November 1962, an advisory group of the Aeronautical Systems Division reviewed it on 22 and 23 June 1964, and the Air Force held a programme review on 13 January 1965. Braslow adds that the Wright Air Development Division chose the WB-66D for minimum cost, a high degree of safety and a short development time, and that the Federal Aviation Administration contributed money. [Butz 1963][ref_butz_1963] gives the sums as 35 million dollars from the Air Force and 2 million from the Federal Aviation Agency.

The Air Force wanted the result for range. An aircraft with the drag of an X-21 wing could loiter far from base or cross an ocean without refuelling, and the 1963 case for the programme was put in those terms in [Butz 1963][ref_butz_1963], promising a lift-to-drag ratio raised from about twenty to more than thirty and an airframe more than forty percent smaller for the same mission. The example given was one model of the B-52, reduced from 400,000 pounds to 225,000 pounds without changing its speed, range, endurance or load, which is a reduction of

$$1 - \frac{225{,}000}{400{,}000} = 0.44$$

or 44 percent. **Those numbers are for an aircraft designed around laminar flow from the start.** They are not what a converted bomber can show, and the difference between the two turns out to matter more than it looks.

## The Aircraft and Its Suction System

The X-21A was a WB-66D with the engines moved and the wing replaced. Two General Electric J79 turbojets, which the cutaway drawing in [Butz 1963][ref_butz_1963] labels YJ79-GE-13, went to pods on the rear fuselage, which made room under the wing for the suction pumps. The new wing spans 93.5 feet with 1,250 square feet of area and 30 degrees of sweep, taken here as quarter-chord sweep, reduced from the 35 degrees of the bomber, in the [Northrop X-21A][ref_x21] reference entry. It was scaled from a [Douglas DC-8][ref_dc8] wing at about forty percent, so that the result would be representative of a large subsonic transport instead of a research shape.

$$\bar{c} = \frac{S}{b} = \frac{1{,}250}{93.5} = 13.37\ \text{ft}$$

$$A\!R = \frac{b^{2}}{S} = \frac{93.5^{2}}{1{,}250} = 6.99$$

The suction surface needs careful description, because it is routinely described wrongly. The wing skin carries spanwise slots running from root to tip, **between 0.0025 and 0.008 inches wide**, comparable with the cutting edge of a razor blade. Beneath each slot a plenum chamber is milled into an inner skin, and from each plenum a large number of small holes is drilled down into deep ducts formed between layers of bonded sandwich structure. Air is drawn through the slots into the plenums, through the holes into the ducts, and along the ducts at speeds reaching about two hundred miles per hour to four small gas turbines, two in each underwing nacelle, one serving the forward wing and one the rear, as [Butz 1963][ref_butz_1963] describes the installation. The skin panels, usually about five feet by twenty, were set in the final assembly jig first and the spars and understructure added behind them, so that the wing was built from the outside in and the outer contour did not follow the structure's tolerances. Butz reports the programme's estimate that such a wing would cost only about twenty percent more to produce than a conventional wing of the same size and weight.

Two numbers make the surface concrete. With leading-edge slots about 0.0035 inches wide on a pitch of 0.75 inches, the values [Joslin 1998b][ref_joslin_1998] gives for the slots that controlled the flow at the attachment line, the open fraction of the skin is

$$\sigma = \frac{w}{p} = \frac{0.0035}{0.75} = 0.00467$$

**The suction surface is 99.5 percent solid.** And since the flow through the skin averages 0.145 feet per second, continuity gives the speed in the slots themselves as

$$v_{\text{slot}} = \frac{v_{w}}{\sigma} = \frac{0.145}{0.00467} = 31\ \text{ft/s}$$

or about 21 miles per hour, against the 200 miles per hour reported in the ducts below. The air does not rush through the slot. The surface behaves as a very slightly porous skin.

**The figure of eight hundred thousand, which reference works such as the [Northrop X-21A][ref_x21] entry give as a count of slots, is a count of those metering holes.** [Butz 1963][ref_butz_1963] is explicit, describing air gathered in small chambers and channelled through pin-size holes, more than 800,000 of them, into the larger ducts. No primary document available here gives a count of the slots themselves, but their spacing bounds it. [Joslin 1998b][ref_joslin_1998] gives typical slot spacings of 1.1, 2.0 and 1.2 inches over 1 to 5, 5 to 40 and 40 to 100 percent of the chord. At the mean chord of 160.4 inches each surface therefore carries about

$$N_{\text{slot}} = \sum_{i} \frac{\Delta (x/c)_{i} \, \bar{c}}{s_{i}} = \frac{0.04 \times 160.4}{1.1} + \frac{0.35 \times 160.4}{2.0} + \frac{0.60 \times 160.4}{1.2} = 5.8 + 28.1 + 80.2 = 114$$

slots, where $\Delta (x/c)_{i}$ is the chordwise extent of each region and $s_{i}$ its slot spacing, so the wing has of the order of a hundred slots on each surface and a little over two hundred in all. The estimate ignores the closer spacing on the attachment line and treats the mean chord as typical. The distinction is not pedantry. A slot is a continuous span-long opening and a hole is a discrete metering orifice, and confusing the two makes the wing sound like a sieve when it is in fact a small number of very fine, very long, very accurately cut openings. The design and construction of such surfaces is treated in [Cliett 1952][research_cliett_1952], [Gregory 1961][research_gregory_1961], [Meade 1977][research_meade_1977], [Klotzsche et al. 1984][research_klotzsche_pearce_1984], [Lineberger 1984][research_lineberger_1984], [Fischler 1986][research_fischler_1986], [Maddalon and Poppen 1986][research_maddalon_poppen_1986], [Goodyear 1987][research_goodyear_1987], [Maddalon and Braslow 1990][research_maddalon_braslow_1990], [Maddalon 1991][research_maddalon_1991], [Traub et al. 2021][research_traub_wolff_2021], [Prasannakumar et al. 2024][research_prasannakumar_2024], [Kashaev et al. 2026][research_kashaev_2026].

## Sizing From First Principles

The natural question is whether the suction power exceeds the drag it saves. **At the suction coefficient assumed here it does not, by a wide margin**, although the local coefficients the X-21 design called for narrow the margin considerably, as the end of this section shows. The calculation deserves care, because its answer removes the obvious explanation for the programme's fate and forces the search elsewhere.

Take a nominal cruise at Mach 0.75 and 40,000 feet. At that altitude the density is $5.851 \times 10^{-4}$ slugs per cubic foot and the speed of sound is 968.1 feet per second, so the flight speed and dynamic pressure are

$$V = M a = 0.75 \times 968.1 = 726.0\ \text{ft/s}$$

$$q = \tfrac{1}{2} \rho V^{2} = 154.2\ \text{lbf/ft}^{2}$$

and at the 83,000 pound gross weight of the [Northrop X-21A][ref_x21] entry the wing is working at

$$C_{L} = \frac{W}{q S} = \frac{83{,}000}{154.2 \times 1{,}250} = 0.431$$

The mean-chord Reynolds number is

$$Re_{\bar{c}} = \frac{V \bar{c}}{\nu} = \frac{726.0 \times 13.37}{5.075 \times 10^{-4}} = 1.913 \times 10^{7}$$

At that Reynolds number the mean skin-friction coefficients on a smooth flat plate are

$$C_{f,\text{turb}} = \frac{0.455}{(\log_{10} Re)^{2.58}} = 0.002713$$

$$C_{f,\text{lam}} = \frac{1.328}{\sqrt{Re}} = 0.000304$$

The ratio of the two sets the size of the prize,

$$\frac{C_{f,\text{lam}}}{C_{f,\text{turb}}} = \frac{0.000304}{0.002713} = 0.112$$

so the laminar boundary layer exerts **11.2 percent** of the turbulent shear and the saving available is

$$\Delta C_{f} = 0.002713 - 0.000304 = 0.002409$$

On the area the programme guaranteed to laminarise, 70 percent of the wing in [Butz 1963][ref_butz_1963], with the exposed wetted area of the wing taken as about 2,222 square feet, that coefficient is worth

$$\Delta D = q \, \Delta C_{f} \, S_{\text{lam}} = 154.2 \times 0.002409 \times 1{,}555 = 578\ \text{lbf}$$

### What the Suction Costs

The suction system ingests a mass flow set by the suction coefficient $C_Q$, the ratio of the velocity through the surface to the freestream velocity,

$$v_{w} = C_{Q} V_{\infty} = 2 \times 10^{-4} \times 726.0 = 0.145\ \text{ft/s}$$

**The suction amounts to no more than that.** The air is drawn through the skin at about a tenth of a mile per hour, and everything the wing achieves follows from removing it. The mass flow is

$$\dot{m} = \rho_{\infty} V_{\infty} C_{Q} S_{\text{wet}}$$

That air arrives in the ducts at a total pressure well below freestream total pressure, because it has passed through a slot narrower than a razor edge and along a duct. To expel it without a momentum penalty the system must restore it, and the ideal work per unit mass is the isentropic compression work. Writing $\Pi$ for the ratio of freestream total pressure to duct total pressure and $\kappa = (\gamma-1)/\gamma$, the shaft power is

$$P = \frac{\dot{m} c_{p} T_{t}}{\eta} \left[ \Pi^{\kappa} - 1 \right]$$

The equivalent drag is that power divided by flight speed, and dividing again by $q S_{\text{wet}}$ gives a drag coefficient on the same basis as the skin friction,

$$C_{D,\text{suction}} = \frac{2 C_{Q} c_{p} T_{t} \left[ \Pi^{\kappa} - 1 \right]}{\eta V^{2}}$$

**Density has cancelled.** Substituting $c_p T = a^{2}/(\gamma-1)$ and $V = M a$ removes the atmosphere entirely and leaves

$$C_{D,\text{suction}} = \frac{2 C_{Q} \left[ \Pi^{\kappa} - 1 \right]}{\eta (\gamma-1) M^{2}} \left( 1 + \frac{\gamma-1}{2} M^{2} \right)$$

**The suction penalty is a function of Mach number and of the machinery, and of nothing else.** It does not depend on altitude at all. An aircraft climbing at fixed Mach number carries exactly the same suction drag coefficient at 20,000 feet as at 50,000, which is not obvious in advance and which removes an entire axis from the design trade.

With a design suction coefficient of $C_Q = 2 \times 10^{-4}$, a duct total-pressure recovery of 0.60 and a combined turbine and compressor efficiency of 0.75,

$$C_{D,\text{suction}} = 4.144 \times 10^{-4}$$

which is **17.2 percent** of the friction saving. Five sixths of the prize survives the pumping.

### How Far From Break-Even

Setting the suction penalty equal to the friction saving gives the suction coefficient at which the system stops paying,

$$C_{Q,\text{crit}} = \frac{\Delta C_{f} \, \eta (\gamma-1) M^{2}}{2 \left[ \Pi^{\kappa} - 1 \right] \left( 1 + \tfrac{\gamma-1}{2} M^{2} \right)} = 1.163 \times 10^{-3}$$

**That is 5.8 times the design value.** In absolute terms, at the nominal condition and over the wing area the programme guaranteed to laminarise, the suction system moves 4.25 pounds of air per second and absorbs about 131 horsepower, while the drag it removes is 578 pounds of force, worth 763 horsepower at flight speed. Written as a ratio of powers,

$$\frac{\Delta D \, V}{P} = \frac{578 \times 726.0}{131 \times 550} = 5.8$$

the same 5.8 as the margin in suction coefficient, and necessarily so, since both sides of the comparison are linear in $C_Q$.

The margin is large enough that the interesting question is not whether it is positive but how it varies. It improves with Mach number, because the suction penalty falls as $1/M^{2}$ while the friction saving is nearly constant.

| Mach | $C_{D,\text{suction}}$ | $\Delta C_{f}$ | penalty as a fraction |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0.40 | 0.001351 | 0.002578 | 0.524 |
| 0.50 | 0.000880 | 0.002518 | 0.350 |
| 0.60 | 0.000624 | 0.002469 | 0.253 |
| 0.70 | 0.000469 | 0.002428 | 0.193 |
| 0.75 | 0.000414 | 0.002409 | 0.172 |
| 0.80 | 0.000369 | 0.002392 | 0.154 |
| 0.85 | 0.000332 | 0.002376 | 0.140 |

**Laminar flow control is a high-speed technology**, and the reason is thermodynamic rather than aerodynamic. The work needed to recompress a unit of captured air is set by temperature, and the kinetic energy available to pay for it goes as the square of speed. For that reason the application was always aimed at fast subsonic transports and why a slow aircraft is a poor candidate however clean its wing.

### The Suction the X-21 Drew

The suction coefficient of $2 \times 10^{-4}$ used above is an assumption, and the record of the programme allows it to be tested. **The local suction coefficients the X-21 design called for were higher.** [Joslin 1998b][ref_joslin_1998], summarising the Northrop final report, gives typical values of $v_{w}/V_{\infty}$ from $5 \times 10^{-4}$ in regions of nearly flat pressure to $10 \times 10^{-4}$ near the leading edge, and adds that at Mach 0.74 and 41,400 feet the measured suction distribution matched the predicted one in shape but exceeded it in level by 50 percent. Since the break-even coefficient above is fixed by the friction saving and the machinery, the margin at any other coefficient is

$$\frac{C_{Q,\text{crit}}}{C_{Q}} = \frac{1.163 \times 10^{-3}}{5 \times 10^{-4}} = 2.3 \qquad \frac{1.163 \times 10^{-3}}{10 \times 10^{-4}} = 1.16$$

Because the penalty is linear in $C_{Q}$, its share of the friction saving rises from 0.172 to $0.172 \times 2.5 = 0.43$ and $0.172 \times 5 = 0.86$. So where the suction was strongest the system was close to paying nothing back, and over the flat-pressure regions that make up most of the chord it returned a little more than twice what it cost.

The same summary reports the system's flow at one condition, 43,000 feet and Mach 0.75, as ranging from 1.94 to 7.18 pounds per second. At 43,000 feet, 3,000 feet into the isothermal layer whose scale height is $H = R T/g = 20{,}805$ feet, the density is

$$\rho_{43} = \rho_{40} \, e^{-3{,}000/H} = 5.851 \times 10^{-4} \times e^{-0.1442} = 5.065 \times 10^{-4}\ \text{slugs/ft}^{3}$$

and if those flows are totals over the whole wetted wing they imply an area-averaged coefficient of

$$\bar{C}_{Q} = \frac{\dot{m}}{\rho_{43} V_{\infty} S_{\text{wet}}} = \frac{7.18/32.174}{5.065 \times 10^{-4} \times 726.0 \times 2{,}222} = 2.7 \times 10^{-4}$$

at the upper figure and $0.74 \times 10^{-4}$ at the lower. **The summary does not say whether those flows are system totals or the flows of individual pumps or regions**, and the two readings differ by a factor of several. Read as totals, they bracket the assumed coefficient and leave a margin of about four at the upper figure. Read against the local design values, the margin is between one and two. The record therefore supports the sign of the conclusion, that suction cost less than it saved, and does not support the factor of six as a measured property of this aeroplane.

## Why the Testbed Could Not Show What Was Being Claimed

The trade above is per unit of laminarised area. The aircraft-level benefit depends on how much of the aeroplane is laminarised, and here the X-21 was structurally limited by being a conversion.

The wing is about 44 percent of the aircraft wetted area. The programme guaranteed laminar flow over 70 percent of the wing, in [Butz 1963][ref_butz_1963], which is 31 percent of the aeroplane.

The zero-lift drag has to be built from its parts, not assumed, because the friction saving must be credited on the same basis the drag is charged. Taking a wetted-area ratio of 4.0 and a form factor of 1.20, which accounts for the thickness of a real wing over a flat plate,

$$C_{D_{0}} = k \, C_{f,\text{turb}} \frac{S_{\text{wet}}}{S} + C_{D,\text{other}} = 1.20 \times 0.002713 \times 4.0 + 0.0050 = 0.01802$$

where the residual 0.0050 is interference, excrescence and everything that is not skin friction. Drag build-up of this kind, and the span-efficiency factor the induced term needs, are period subjects with a settled literature, in [Report No. 349. 1930][research_report_no_1930], [Oswald 1932][research_w_bailey_oswald_1932], [Lockwood Taylor 1942][research_lockwood_taylor_1942], [Sibert 1943][research_sibert_1943], [von Kármán and Tsien 1945][research_von_karman_tsien_1945], [Lakshminarayana 1962][research_lakshminarayana_1962], [Frost and Rutherford 1963][research_frost_rutherford_1963], [Sanders 1965][research_sanders_1965], [Gilman and Burdges 1967][research_gilman_burdges_1967], [Lundry 1967][research_lundry_1967], [Snyder and Zumwalt 1969][research_snyder_zumwalt_1969], [Hancock 1971][research_hancock_1971], [Morita 1971][research_morita_1971], [Hancock 1972][research_hancock_1972], [Letcher 1972][research_letcher_1972], [Moriya 1972][research_moriya_1972], [Mamada and Ando 1973][research_mamada_ando_1973], [Mamada and Ando 1974][research_mamada_ando_1974], [Sears 1974][research_sears_1974], [Deyoung 1980][research_deyoung_1980], [Smith and Kroo 1990][research_smith_kroo_1990], [Straussfogel and Maughmer 1991][research_straussfogel_maughmer_1991], [Smith 1996a][research_smith_1996_2]. **The efficiency factor and the performance charts that go with it are [Oswald 1932][research_w_bailey_oswald_1932]**, whose span-efficiency factor is the 0.80 this article uses for the induced drag and is still called by his name. With an Oswald efficiency of 0.80 the induced drag at the cruise lift coefficient is

$$C_{D_{i}} = \frac{C_{L}^{2}}{\pi A\!R\, e} = \frac{0.431^{2}}{\pi \times 6.99 \times 0.80} = 0.01055$$

$$C_{D} = C_{D_{0}} + C_{D_{i}} = 0.02857 \qquad \frac{L}{D} = \frac{C_{L}}{C_{D}} = 15.07$$

**That decomposition also settles the claim the article opened with.** The friction share of total drag is

$$\frac{k \, C_{f,\text{turb}} S_{\text{wet}} / S}{C_{D}} = \frac{0.01302}{0.02857} = 0.456$$

The assertion that about half a transport's fuel goes to overcoming skin friction came from the literature. This number came from geometry and a flat-plate correlation. **They agree to about nine percent, from two directions that share nothing**, and that agreement carries more weight than either statement alone.

Now the saving. Over the laminarised wetted area the friction falls by $k \Delta C_{f}$ and the suction charges $C_{D,\text{suction}}$, which is a power and carries no form factor, so

$$\Delta D = \left( k \, \Delta C_{f} - C_{D,\text{suction}} \right) q \, S_{\text{lam}}$$

$$\frac{\Delta D}{D} = 0.108 \qquad \frac{L}{D} = 16.89$$

By the Breguet relation,

$$R = \frac{V}{c_{t}} \frac{L}{D} \ln \frac{W_{i}}{W_{f}}$$

range is proportional to lift-to-drag ratio at fixed speed, specific fuel consumption and fuel fraction, so **the aeroplane as flown was worth about twelve percent in range**. The relation itself is textbook material, not journal material, treated in [Anderson 1999 Aircraft Performance and Design][book_anderson_1999], and the cruise-optimisation literature around it is narrow, being [Hale 1976][research_hale_1976], [Guynn 2011][research_guynn_2011]. **That literature is thin under this name**, because the word range in an aeronautical archive mostly means the distance to a target or a ballistic test facility rather than the distance an aircraft can cruise.

| laminarised | fraction of wetted area | drag reduction | $L/D$ | range gain |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| wing, 70 percent | 0.311 | 0.108 | 16.89 | 0.121 |
| wing, 95 percent | 0.422 | 0.146 | 17.66 | 0.172 |
| wing entire | 0.444 | 0.154 | 17.82 | 0.182 |
| wing and tail | 0.618 | 0.215 | 19.18 | 0.273 |
| wing, tail, nacelles | 0.758 | 0.263 | 20.44 | 0.356 |
| everything | 1.000 | 0.347 | 23.07 | 0.531 |

### What the 1963 Claim Requires

**The case for laminar flow control was a doubling of range and a lift-to-drag ratio above thirty**, in [Butz 1963][ref_butz_1963].

Take the last row of that table seriously for a moment and laminarise the entire aeroplane, fuselage included, which is impossible but bounds the argument. The zero-lift drag becomes

$$C_{D_{0}}^{\ \text{lam}} = 1.20 \times 0.000304 \times 4.0 + 0.0050 = 0.00646$$

and adding induced drag and the suction charge over the whole wetted area,

$$C_{D}^{\ \text{best}} = 0.00646 + 0.01055 + 4.0 \times 4.144 \times 10^{-4} = 0.01866 \quad \Rightarrow \quad \frac{L}{D} = 23.1$$

**Twenty-three, not thirty, with every square foot of the aeroplane laminar.** The reason is visible in the arithmetic. A lift-to-drag ratio of thirty at this lift coefficient allows a total drag coefficient of

$$C_{D} = \frac{C_{L}}{30} = \frac{0.431}{30} = 0.01435$$

and the induced drag alone is 0.01055, which is **73 percent of the entire budget**. Induced drag is set by the planform and is untouched by anything done to the boundary layer. Inverting for the aspect ratio that would leave room,

$$A\!R = \frac{C_{L}^{2}}{\pi e \left( C_{D} - C_{D_{0}}^{\ \text{lam}} - C_{D,\text{suction}} S_{\text{wet}}/S \right)} = 11.8$$

against the X-21's 6.99.

**So laminar flow control does not merely clean a wing. It moves the optimum planform.** Once friction is removed, induced drag dominates, and the aircraft that collects the full benefit wants a much longer, thinner wing than the one the X-21 inherited from a bomber. This gives a second and independent reason the testbed could not demonstrate the case, stronger than the wetted-area argument because no amount of suction on this planform can reach the number. Nothing in that table reaches it, and the last row already assumes the impossible. The gap does not mean the case was in error. It measures the difference between laminarising an existing aeroplane and designing one around laminar flow, where the wing shrinks, the fuel load shrinks, the structure lightens and the sizing spiral runs inward. The transport design studies that worked this through are in [Marino et al. 1975][research_marino_1975], [Povinelli et al. 1976][research_povinelli_1976], [Bonner et al. 1977][research_bonner_1977], [Gratzer and George-Falvy 1978][research_gratzer_george_falvy_1978], [Klineberg 1978][research_klineberg_1978], [Sturgeon 1978b][research_sturgeon_1978], [Aircraft Energy Efficiency Program 1979][research_aircraft_energy_1979], [Klineberg 1979][research_klineberg_1979], [Swift and Mungur 1979][research_swift_mungur_1979], [Wright 1979][research_wright_1979], [Evaluation of Laminar Flow 1980][research_evaluation_of_1980], [Wagner et al. 1984][research_wagner_1984], [Middleton et al. 1985][research_middleton_1985], [Wagner 1986][research_wagner_1986_2], [Lange 1988][research_lange_1988], [Parikh and Nagel 1990][research_parikh_nagel_1990], [Arcara et al. 1991][research_arcara_1991], [Fischer and Vemuru 1991][research_fischer_vemuru_1991].

**The programme knew this in 1963.** [Butz 1963][ref_butz_1963] lists among the features of a future laminar flow control aircraft a wing of at least fifty percent more area than a conventional aircraft of the same gross weight, because once friction drag is removed from the sizing the span can be increased to cut the drag due to lift without an undue structural penalty. The programme thus stated the planform argument above while the X-21 was flying, which confirms that the thirty was always a figure for a different wing.

**So the X-21 was an instrument that could not measure the quantity being argued about.** It could show that the flow stayed laminar, and it did. It could not show the aircraft-level benefit, because the aircraft-level benefit belonged to a different aeroplane.

## What Went Wrong at the Attachment Line

On a swept wing the flow divides along a line near the leading edge, and the boundary layer along that line is fed spanwise from the root outward. If it becomes turbulent anywhere, the turbulence can propagate along the line and contaminate everything outboard of it. **No amount of suction downstream helps, because the flow arrives already turbulent.** This is spanwise contamination, treated in [Cumpsty and Head 1967b][research_cumpsty_head_1967], [Cumpsty and Head 1969][research_cumpsty_head_1969], [Cebeci 1974][research_cebeci_1974], [Poll 1979][research_poll_1979], [Cumpsty and Head 1980][research_cumpsty_head_1980], [Hall et al. 1984][research_hall_1984], [Hall and Mackerrell 1988][research_hall_mackerrell_1988], [Holmes 1991][research_holmes_1991], [Miley et al. 1992][research_naval_k_agatwal_1992], [Joslin 1996a][research_joslin_1996_2], [Joslin 1996b][research_joslin_1996], [Smith 1996b][research_smith_1996], [Poll 2007][research_poll_2007], [Reed and Saric 2013][research_reed_saric_2013], [Yatskih et al. 2016][research_yatskih_2016], [Manuilovich 2017a][research_manuilovich_2017], [Manuilovich 2017b][research_manuilovich_2017_2], [Liu 2019][research_liu_2019], [Fedorov and Egorov 2021][research_fedorov_egorov_2021], [Xi et al. 2021][research_xi_2021], [Xi et al. 2025][research_xi_2025].

The governing parameter is an attachment-line Reynolds number formed from the spanwise velocity and the boundary-layer scale at the line. It is better assembled than quoted, because each part is a separate physical statement. The flow along the line moves at the spanwise component,

$$W_{e} = V_{\infty} \sin\Lambda = 726.0 \times \sin 33.2^{\circ} = 397.5\ \text{ft/s}$$

The boundary layer there does not grow, because it is continuously stretched by the chordwise acceleration away from the line, which for a leading edge of radius $r$ is

$$\left. \frac{d U_{e}}{ds} \right|_{\text{al}} = \frac{2 V_{\infty} \cos\Lambda}{r} = 7{,}385\ \text{s}^{-1}$$

That balance fixes a constant thickness scale,

$$\ell = \sqrt{\frac{\nu}{d U_{e}/ds}} = 2.62 \times 10^{-4}\ \text{ft} = 0.0031\ \text{in}$$

**Three thousandths of an inch, the width of a slot.** The Reynolds number on that scale is

$$\bar{R} = \frac{W_{e} \ell}{\nu} = \sin\Lambda \sqrt{\frac{V_{\infty} r}{2 \nu \cos\Lambda}}$$

The sweep in that expression is the **leading-edge** sweep, not the quarter-chord sweep quoted for a planform. With a taper ratio of 0.30 the X-21 wing carries

$$\tan\Lambda_{LE} = \tan\Lambda_{c/4} + \frac{1-\lambda}{A\!R(1+\lambda)} \quad \Rightarrow \quad \Lambda_{LE} = 33.2^{\circ}$$

Below about 100 an introduced disturbance decays. Above about 245 it is sustained and propagates. Between the two, whether the attachment line stays laminar depends on the size of the disturbance introduced, in the experiments reviewed by [Joslin 1998b][ref_joslin_1998]. Evaluating along the span, with leading-edge radius taken as 0.008 of the local chord,

| span station | chord, ft | $\bar{R}$ |
|---|---|---|
| root | 20.57 | 205 |
| 0.3 | 16.25 | 183 |
| 0.5 | 13.37 | 166 |
| 0.7 | 10.49 | 147 |
| tip | 6.17 | 113 |

**The X-21 sat in the ambiguous band along its entire span, and highest at the root.** That is the worst place to be. In the safe regime disturbances die whatever the wing does, and in the hopeless regime nothing can be laminar. In the band between, the outcome depends on how large a disturbance the wing root, the fuselage junction or an accumulation of insects happens to present. The programme reported that behaviour, in [Braslow 1999][book_braslow_1999], and it is the behaviour hardest to design against.

The result is sensitive to the leading-edge radius, which is assumed here and not measured. At a radius of 0.012 chord the root value rises to 252 and crosses the sustaining threshold outright. **The X-21's wing root therefore sits on the boundary of attachment-line contamination, and which side of it depends on a geometric detail of about one percent of chord.** The most visible external feature of the aeroplane, a large fairing over the forward wing-root region, exists to tailor the pressure field there, in [Butz 1963][ref_butz_1963].

**The X-21's design rule expresses the same thing in different variables.** The analysis behind the wing required the momentum-thickness Reynolds number on the attachment line to stay below 100, according to [Joslin 1998b][ref_joslin_1998]. The two parameters are related through the spanwise velocity profile of the swept attachment-line flow, the swept Hiemenz solution, whose spanwise momentum thickness in units of $\ell$ is

$$\frac{\theta}{\ell} = \int_{0}^{\infty} g(\eta) \left[ 1 - g(\eta) \right] d\eta = 0.404 \qquad \Rightarrow \qquad R_{\theta} = 0.404 \, \bar{R}$$

where $g = w/W_{e}$ is the spanwise velocity ratio, obtained by integrating $f''' + f f'' + 1 - f'^{2} = 0$ and $g'' + f g' = 0$ numerically, and $\eta = y/\ell$. The design limit is therefore

$$\bar{R} < \frac{100}{0.404} = 247$$

matching the upper threshold of 245 quoted above to within the precision of either. The experimental basis of that threshold, from Cumpsty and Head in 1969 to [Poll 1979][research_poll_1979], is reviewed by Joslin. At the root the table gives $R_{\theta} = 0.404 \times 205 = 83$. **The wing met its design criterion at every station along the span**, so the contamination it suffered was not a failure of the criterion. It came from large disturbances, the turbulent fuselage boundary layer above all, and for large disturbances the relevant threshold is the lower one near 100, which every station exceeded.

The cure was accordingly to remove the disturbance rather than to change the wing. The review group of October 1965, in its report reproduced in [Braslow 1999][book_braslow_1999], concluded that apart from some uncertainty about contamination by turbulence from the fuselage the flights had verified the method of aerodynamic design, that the cause of the leading-edge trouble had been believed found by the middle of 1964, and that two means of controlling contamination from the fuselage had been developed and proven in flight, a special fence and a suction leading-edge portion containing vertical slots. [Joslin 1998b][ref_joslin_1998] lists a fence, vertical slots and a gutter at the wing-fuselage junction.

The related instability, in which the spanwise pressure gradient on a swept wing drives a crossflow profile with an inflection point, is the reason sweep is hostile to laminar flow in the first place, and its treatment is [Dagenhart et al. 1989][research_dagenhart_1989], [Mousseux et al. 1989][research_mousseux_1989], [Reed 1989][research_reed_1989].

## Surface Tolerance

A slotted suction wing must be smoother than a conventional one, because roughness and waviness trigger transition that suction downstream cannot undo. The tolerance literature is [Van Driest and Blumer 1961][research_van_driest_blumer_1961], [Lyons and Levensteins 1962][research_lyons_1962], [Nagamatsu et al. 1964][research_nagamatsu_1964], [Merkle et al. 1974][research_merkle_1974], [Boudreau 1978][research_boudreau_1978], [Leventhal and Reshotko 1981][research_leventhal_reshotko_1981], [Kendall 1982][research_kendall_1982], [Bertolotti 1993][research_bertolotti_1993], [Masad 1996][research_masad_1996], [Saric 1997][research_saric_1997], [Stewart and Simpson 2005][research_stewart_simpson_2005], [Saric 2008][research_saric_2008], [Zhong 2009][research_zhong_2009], [Saric 2010][research_saric_2010], [Borodulin et al. 2015][research_borodulin_2015], [Crouch et al. 2015][research_crouch_2015], [Malik et al. 2015][research_malik_2015], [Ustinov and Kachanov 2021][research_ustinov_kachanov_2021], [Borodulin et al. 2022][research_borodulin_2022], [Xu and Wu 2022][research_xu_wu_2022], [Zoppini et al. 2022b][research_zoppini_2022], [Nakagawa et al. 2023][research_nakagawa_2023], [Zheng et al. 2026][research_zheng_2026].

**The tolerance is computable and it is brutal.** A three-dimensional roughness element trips a laminar boundary layer when the Reynolds number formed on its height and the velocity at that height passes a critical value of about 600, in [Hall 1964][ref_hall_1964]. Near the wall the Blasius profile is linear, $u/U_{\infty} = 0.332\,\eta$, so

$$Re_{k} = \frac{u_{k} k}{\nu} = \frac{0.332\, k^{2} V_{\infty}^{3/2}}{\nu^{3/2} x^{1/2}} = 600$$

Solving for the admissible height at a station one foot behind the leading edge,

$$k_{\text{crit}} = \left( \frac{600 \, \nu^{3/2} x^{1/2}}{0.332 \, V_{\infty}^{3/2}} \right)^{1/2} = 0.0123\ \text{in}$$

**About a hundredth of an inch, and it scales as $x^{1/4}$, so it is smaller further forward**.

That criterion is not a modern reconstruction. It was established in these same years. Its dense literature is gathered in the cluster on roughness, waviness and surface tolerance below, and the criteria of that period include [Tani and Hama 1953][research_tani_hama_1953], [A Low-Speed Experimental Investigation 1959][research_a_low_speed_1959], [Dommett 1959][research_dommett_1959], [Gibbings 1959][research_gibbings_1959], [Smith and Clutter 1959][research_smith_clutter_1959], [Van Driest and McCAULEY 1960][research_van_driest_mccauley_1960], [Potter and Whitfield 1961][research_potter_whitfield_1961], [Lyons and Levensteins 1962][research_lyons_1962], [Review of the Effect 1962][research_review_of_1962], [Holloway and Sterrett 1963][research_holloway_sterrett_1963]. **The simplified method for the critical height of distributed roughness particles is [Braslow and Knox 1958][research_braslow_knox_1958]**, so the man who wrote the history this article relies on for its narrative also wrote the criterion it uses for its arithmetic. For comparison the leading-edge slots are 0.0035 inches wide, in [Joslin 1998b][ref_joslin_1998], so the surface had to be held flat to within about three slot widths.

**Much of the programme's first two years went here.** The wings were built to close tolerances and the tolerances were still not close enough. Discontinuities at the spanwise wing splices were large enough to trip the boundary layer, putty used to fair them chipped away in flight, and the resulting roughness did the tripping instead. The problem was solved, but it consumed the schedule, in [Braslow 1999][book_braslow_1999]. [Joslin 1998b][ref_joslin_1998], drawing on Fowell and Antonatos, gives the mechanism. The wings were built from many panels, the splices between them were filled with epoxy to meet the step and waviness tolerances at the high unit Reynolds number, and the epoxy cracked and chipped under the wing loading and the temperature changes of flight. The cause was traced to the bonding process, which was changed until the fills held.

**The tolerances the programme arrived at can be set against the criterion above.** For Mach 0.8 at 45,000 feet the Northrop final report, as [Joslin 1998b][ref_joslin_1998] gives it, allows forward-facing steps of 0.02 inches, rearward-facing steps of 0.009 inches and spanwise gaps 0.25 inches wide, and requires single waves to have an amplitude less than 0.004 of their wavelength. Five thousand feet into the isothermal layer the density is $\rho_{40} e^{-5{,}000/H} = 4.601 \times 10^{-4}$ slugs per cubic foot, the viscosity is unchanged, and the kinematic viscosity rises in proportion to $6.454 \times 10^{-4}$ square feet per second. Since the admissible height above goes as $\nu^{3/4} V^{-3/4}$,

$$k_{\text{crit},45} = 0.0123 \left( \frac{6.454}{5.075} \right)^{3/4} \left( \frac{726.0}{774.5} \right)^{3/4} = 0.0140\ \text{in}$$

one foot behind the leading edge at $V = 0.8 \times 968.1 = 774.5$ feet per second. **The allowed steps bracket the three-dimensional criterion**, rearward steps tighter than it and forward steps looser. A step is a two-dimensional disturbance with its own criterion, so the agreement is one of scale rather than an identity, but it shows the arithmetic and the hardware were working on the same thousandths of an inch. The October 1965 review found that a previously suggested criterion for single surface waves had been confirmed in flight, and that vibration of isolated panels at a discrete frequency did not disturb the laminar flow, in [Braslow 1999][book_braslow_1999]. [Joslin 1998b][ref_joslin_1998] adds that the wing tolerated a gap 0.125 inches wide and 0.18 inches deep at 60 percent chord on the lower outboard wing.

## Atmospheric Particles

Flying in or near cirrus, the X-21 lost laminar flow. On emerging from the cloud it regained it immediately, in [Braslow 1999][book_braslow_1999]. The Northrop study of the problem, [Hall 1964][ref_hall_1964], opens with the observation that numerous flights had shown total loss of laminar flow control within or near visible cloud, and erratic performance in light haze when the humidity was relatively high. The mechanism is that ice crystals passing through the boundary layer generate turbulent wedges, and the effect is not a contamination of the surface but a continuous seeding of the flow.

**Both halves of that observation follow from one calculation.** A point disturbance in a laminar boundary layer does not simply trip the flow at that point. It produces a turbulent spot that grows as it convects, spreading laterally at a roughly constant angle, and the mechanism and its measurement are [Schubauer and Klebanoff 1955][research_schubauer_klebanoff_1955], [Sokolov et al. 1980][research_sokolov_1980], [Glezer et al. 1986][research_glezer_1986], [Henningson et al. 1987][research_henningson_1987], [Henningson et al. 1988][research_henningson_1988], [Seifert et al. 1992][research_seifert_1992], [Singer and Joslin 1995][research_singer_joslin_1995], [Simon and Boyle 1998][research_simon_boyle_1998], [LaGraff 2001][research_lagraff_2001], [Blackwelder et al. 2007][research_blackwelder_2007], [Clark et al. 2007][research_clark_2007], [Fehrs and Kaiser 2025][research_fehrs_kaiser_2025]. The spreading half-angle is about ten degrees, in [Schubauer and Klebanoff 1955][research_schubauer_klebanoff_1955], so a particle entering at the leading edge contaminates

$$A_{w} = \bar{c}^{\,2} \tan\alpha = 13.37^{2} \times \tan 10^{\circ} = 31.5\ \text{ft}^{2}$$

which means that

$$n = \frac{S}{A_{w}} = \frac{1{,}250}{31.5} \approx 40$$

wedges are enough to blanket the wing. The laminar boundary layer is thin,

$$\delta = \frac{5 \bar{c}}{\sqrt{Re}} = 0.0153\ \text{ft} = 0.18\ \text{in}$$

so the rate at which particles enter it across the span is

$$\dot{N} = N V_{\infty} b \, \delta$$

At the cirrus number density of ten thousand crystals per cubic metre assumed here, which is low for cirrus as shown below, that is about 294,000 encounters per second. **The wing is blanketed in roughly 140 microseconds.** On leaving the cloud the wedges convect away in

$$t = \frac{\bar{c}}{V_{\infty}} = \frac{13.37}{726.0} = 18\ \text{ms}$$

**So the loss is instantaneous with five orders of magnitude to spare, and the recovery is instantaneous because nothing has been left behind on the surface.** That distinguishes an ice crystal from an insect, and explains why the two problems needed entirely different answers.

**The analysis in [Hall 1964][ref_hall_1964] supplies what the assumed density lacks.** Working at Mach 0.75 and at 25,000 and 40,000 feet, the same nominal condition used throughout this article, he took cirrus crystals to be hexagonal columns with a length 2.5 times their diameter and found a critical size at 25,000 feet of 17 by 42 micrometres, below which a crystal is too small to start a turbulent spot. [Joslin 1998b][ref_joslin_1998] gives the critical diameters as 17 and 32 micrometres at the two altitudes. Hall's typical cirrus condition is columns 100 micrometres long and 40 wide at an ice content of 0.01 grams per cubic metre. Treating each column as a cylinder of ice of density $\rho_{\text{ice}} = 917$ kilograms per cubic metre, the number density is

$$N = \frac{\text{IWC}}{\rho_{\text{ice}} \, \tfrac{\pi}{4} d^{2} L} = \frac{0.01\ \text{g/m}^{3}}{917{,}000\ \text{g/m}^{3} \times \tfrac{\pi}{4} \times (40 \times 10^{-6})^{2} \times 100 \times 10^{-6}\ \text{m}^{3}} = 8.7 \times 10^{4}\ \text{m}^{-3}$$

where IWC is the ice water content, nearly nine times the density assumed above, and every one of those crystals is well above the critical size. The conclusion that the wing is blanketed at once is therefore conservative.

**The same model shows why haze mattered.** Hall defined the threshold of erratic operation as a time-averaged turbulent area of ten percent of the wing. If wedges arrive at random at rate $\dot{N}$, each covering $A_{w}$ for the convection time $t$, the time-averaged turbulent fraction is

$$\phi = 1 - \exp \left( - \frac{\dot{N} A_{w} t}{S} \right)$$

and setting $\phi = 0.10$ gives

$$\dot{N}_{10} = \frac{-\ln 0.9 \times 1{,}250}{31.5 \times 0.0184} = 227\ \text{s}^{-1} \qquad N_{10} = \frac{\dot{N}_{10}}{V_{\infty} b \, \delta} = \frac{227}{29.4\ \text{m}^{3}/\text{s}} = 7.7\ \text{m}^{-3}$$

with the volume swept by the boundary layer, $V_{\infty} b \delta = 726.0 \times 93.5 \times 0.0153 = 1{,}039$ cubic feet per second, converted to cubic metres. **About eight crystals of critical size in each cubic metre are enough to make laminar flow erratic on this crude model**, four orders of magnitude below a typical cirrus, so air that looks clear can still carry enough of them. Hall reports that pattern, with total loss in cloud where the visibility was several thousand feet and erratic, partially degraded performance in haze through which flight test engineers and pilots could see 30 to 60 miles horizontally.

**The recovery observed in flight was slower than the convection time.** The review group recorded that laminarity lost in cloud was reestablished a few seconds after return to clear air, in [Braslow 1999][book_braslow_1999], against the 18 milliseconds computed above. Since the wing itself clears in milliseconds, the seconds are most plausibly the time taken to leave the diffuse edge of a cloud and the haze around it, which is an inference rather than a finding of the programme. The F-94A glove a decade earlier regained laminar flow within 30 seconds of leaving cloud, in [Joslin 1998b][ref_joslin_1998].

**The claim that the data did not exist is checkable, and it holds up.** Hall wrote in 1964 that quantitative data from sampling of ice particles in natural clouds was practically nil, and took his typical crystal sizes from three references that disagreed in magnitude. Measuring ice-crystal concentration in cloud is a subject of its own, in [Mossop and Ono 1969][research_mossop_ono_1969], [Mossop and Hallett 1974][research_mossop_hallett_1974], [Davis and Fischer 1983][research_davis_fischer_1983], [Baumgardner et al. 2014][research_baumgardner_2014], [Huang 2021][research_huang_2021], and that literature amounts to a handful of campaigns, not the climatology an operator would need. The quantity the X-21 required was not a concentration in one cloud on one day. It was a distribution over altitude, season and route, and **measuring it would have taken a different and much larger programme than anyone had run**.

**This literature is very small.** Work in the archives and journals connecting atmospheric ice particles to laminar flow is essentially [Davis 1982][research_davis_1982], [Davis and Fischer 1983][research_davis_fischer_1983], [Jasperson et al. 1984a][research_jasperson_nastrom_1984], [Jasperson et al. 1984b][research_jasperson_nastron_1984], [Davis et al. 1986][research_davis_1986], [Davis et al. 1987][research_davis_maddalon_1987], [Davis et al. 1989][research_davis_maddalon_1989], [Pohya et al. 2019][research_pohya_2019], for the reason the programme itself gave. There was no statistical description of particle size and concentration as a function of altitude, season and geography, so the operational significance could not be computed, in [Braslow 1999][book_braslow_1999]. **A theory was produced, in [Hall 1964][ref_hall_1964], and the data to apply it did not exist.** Hall recommended that further work be predominantly experimental, by controlled laboratory tests and by flight tests carrying instruments to sample the ice particles. [Joslin 1998b][ref_joslin_1998] records that the cloud encounters of NASA's JetStar leading-edge flight tests in the 1980s, which did carry a particle spectrometer, agreed with Hall's criteria.

## Dependent Systems

The suction pumps are not free of the airframe. The four turbines sit in nacelles under the wing, but their ducting occupies volume inside a wing that must also carry fuel and structure, and the ducts are large because a small duct has its own boundary-layer losses, a pumping penalty on the pumping system, in [Butz 1963][ref_butz_1963].

**The duct size is a check on the suction coefficient from outside the model.** The mass flow over the guaranteed area is 0.132 slugs per second, the contemporary description reports duct speeds reaching 200 miles per hour, and the duct runs at roughly the recovered density, so

$$A_{\text{duct}} = \frac{\dot{m}}{\rho_{d} V_{d}} = \frac{0.132}{3.51 \times 10^{-4} \times 293} = 1.28\ \text{ft}^{2}$$

which is 185 square inches in total, or about 46 square inches in each of four ducts, near enough seven inches square. **That fits inside a wing of 13.4 foot chord without difficulty**, and the figure comes from a reported duct velocity and a computed mass flow that share no input. The pumping installation and its ducting are treated in [Maddalon and Braslow 1990][research_maddalon_braslow_1990], [High Reynolds Number Hybrid 1999b, High Reynolds Number Hybrid Lamin][research_high_reynolds_1999_2], [Prasannakumar et al. 2024][research_prasannakumar_2024], a small literature for a component that decides whether the scheme is worth installing. Had the assumed suction coefficient been an order of magnitude wrong, the duct would not have fitted in the wing. The turbojets themselves were moved to the rear fuselage to make room under the wing for the suction pumps, in [Butz 1963][ref_butz_1963], and that relocation gives the aeroplane its appearance.

**The suction was metered finely and the noise was kept out deliberately.** [Joslin 1998b][ref_joslin_1998] records 96 suction control valves employed to control the suction in the slots independently, and a system that operated between 85 and 130 percent of the nominal design flow so that the unproven method of estimating the flow could be tested. Engine noise can trip a laminar boundary layer, so a movable plug in each engine inlet set up a standing shock wave that kept the compressor noise from reaching the wing, in [Butz 1963][ref_butz_1963]. The 1965 reviewers found that laminar flow persisted at external sound levels about 6 decibels above the established criterion, that noise inside the suction ducts did no harm unless a duct was driven at a resonance large enough to disturb the flow outside the slots, that atmospheric turbulence strong enough to change the angle of attack by one degree either way and to produce 0.3 g at the centre of gravity did not disturb the laminar flow, and that small changes of flight condition, of about 0.04 in lift coefficient or 0.03 in Mach number, did not affect it appreciably, in [Braslow 1999][book_braslow_1999]. Joslin adds that slot Reynolds numbers of 120 to 140 produced disturbances at the slot that dominated any noise from the ducts, so the slots were held below about 100.

The friction the exercise is aimed at is the subject of the cluster on skin friction and the turbulent boundary layer below, with foundations in [Vessey 1935][research_vessey_1935], [Young 1949][research_young_1949], [Rubesin and Johnson 1949][research_rubesin_johnson_1949], [Cope 1952][research_cope_1952], [Lee and Faget 1956][research_lee_faget_1956], [Spalding and Chi 1964][research_spalding_chi_1964], [White and Christoph 1972][research_white_christoph_1972]. The alternative is to shape a wing so that the pressure gradient holds the flow laminar without any suction at all, which needs no plumbing but constrains the aerodynamics severely. Its early flight experiments are [Carmichael 1979][research_carmichael_1979], [Montoya et al. 1981][research_montoya_1981], [Holmes et al. 1984][research_holmes_1984], [Wentz et al. 1984][research_wentz_1984], [Holmes et al. 1985][research_holmes_1985], [Runyan et al. 1987][research_runyan_bielak_1987], [Horstmann et al. 1990b][research_horstmann_redeker_1990], [Holmes and Obara 1992][research_holmes_obara_1992].

## The Flight Test Record

First flight was on 18 April 1963 from Edwards Air Force Base, in the [Northrop X-21A][ref_x21] reference entry, and [Braslow 1999][book_braslow_1999] places the start of flight research in April 1963. Two aircraft were converted, serials 55-0408 and 55-0410. By August 1963 the flights had covered altitudes from 8,000 to 45,000 feet and speeds up to Mach 0.8, in [Butz 1963][ref_butz_1963]. [Joslin 1998b][ref_joslin_1998] gives the envelope as Mach 0.3 to 0.8 at altitudes from 5,000 to 44,000 feet, and the programme as more than 200 flights with the laminar flow control system operating.

**The progress was real and slow.** When the suction system was first switched on, about the outer twelve percent of the wing went laminar. By the middle of 1963 that had reached roughly the outer half, through adjustments to pumping pressures and to the flow distribution among the leading-edge slots, and the project managers were already persuaded that closer slot spacing would be needed on the inboard leading edges, in [Butz 1963][ref_butz_1963]. By the end of the programme, in [Braslow 1999][book_braslow_1999], laminar flow was being obtained over more than ninety-five percent of the area intended for laminarisation.

### The Laminar Run, Year by Year

The chordwise results are better compared as the Reynolds number of the laminar run, the product of the laminar fraction of the chord and the chord Reynolds number,

$$Re_{x} = \frac{x_{\text{lam}}}{c} \, Re_{c}$$

[Joslin 1998b][ref_joslin_1998] gives the progress on the mean aerodynamic chord as follows.

| period | laminar chord | $Re_{c}$ | $Re_{x}$ |
|---|---|---|---|
| end of 1963 | nearly 0.60 | $20 \times 10^{6}$ | $12 \times 10^{6}$ |
| 1964 | 0.70 | $20 \times 10^{6}$ | $14 \times 10^{6}$ |
| 1964 | 0.30 to 0.55 | $30 \times 10^{6}$ | up to $16.5 \times 10^{6}$ |
| 1965 | 0.96 | $20 \times 10^{6}$ | $19.2 \times 10^{6}$ |
| 1965 | 0.81 | $30 \times 10^{6}$ | $24.3 \times 10^{6}$ |
| 1965 | 0.59 | $40 \times 10^{6}$ | $23.6 \times 10^{6}$ |

**The run Reynolds number roughly doubled in two years and then stopped growing with chord Reynolds number.** In 1965 the run at $30 \times 10^{6}$ and at $40 \times 10^{6}$ is the same to within four percent, the pattern a fixed limit on the run, rather than on the fraction of chord, would produce. The group's report gave laminar runs up to a length Reynolds number of 46 million on some parts of the wing, against consistently obtained maxima of less than half that, below 23 million, the year before, and judged that design procedures suitable for 60 million were known but would need flight verification. It also recorded that some direct measurements of overall drag by speed-power methods agreed well with prediction, in [Braslow 1999][book_braslow_1999].

The chord Reynolds numbers are consistent with the geometry used in this article. With the assumed taper ratio of 0.30 the root chord is $c_{r} = 2S/[b(1+\lambda)] = 20.57$ feet and the mean aerodynamic chord is

$$\bar{c}_{\text{MAC}} = \tfrac{2}{3} c_{r} \frac{1 + \lambda + \lambda^{2}}{1 + \lambda} = \tfrac{2}{3} \times 20.57 \times \frac{1.39}{1.30} = 14.66\ \text{ft}$$

so at Mach 0.70 and 40,000 feet the chord Reynolds number is $1.913 \times 10^{7} \times (0.70/0.75) \times (14.66/13.37) = 1.96 \times 10^{7}$, the $20 \times 10^{6}$ of Joslin's example flight at that condition, on which 74 percent of the upper surface and 61 percent of the lower were laminar.

Set against the programme guarantee of seventy percent of the wing, that is a success. **Set against the purpose of the aeroplane, it is one objective out of three.**

### The Review Group of October 1965

The review group met on 14 and 15 October 1965 at the Norair Division in Hawthorne, California, and dated its report 8 November 1965. It was chaired by Holt Ashley and signed by members from NASA, the Federal Aviation Agency, Lockheed, Douglas, Boeing, North American Aviation and Colorado State University, and its report is reproduced in [Braslow 1999][book_braslow_1999]. Braslow's narrative says that the third objective, service experience comparable to an operational aircraft, had not even been initiated, because the effort had gone into surface tolerance and spanwise contamination. **The report's own wording is milder.** It says that the programme had not been able completely to address the operational questions because of difficulties with the basic aerodynamic design, that those difficulties had been overcome, and that the programme should now be directed toward answering them.

Those questions were specific. Can the structure be built to the roughness and waviness criteria, and at what weight and complexity? How far does the surface deteriorate in service? What does it take to keep the wing clean of insects, mud and rain on a routine basis? How tolerant is laminar flow of rain, sleet, ice crystals, cloud and gusts? And what do manoeuvres, off-design lift and non-optimum suction do to it? The group judged an operational suitability evaluation the minimum effort that could justify further funds, recommended that maintainability data be collected to Air Force standard procedures so that availability could be compared with non-laminar aircraft, and asked that the modified aircraft go to the Air Force Flight Test Center well ahead of Northrop's schedule. It asked for serious consideration of a wholly new set of metal upper wing surfaces needing no filler, judged closing out the flight programme at once particularly undesirable because a large military transport with laminar flow control, perhaps drawn from the C-5A line, was under consideration, and called for systems analyses comparing laminar flow control with better engines, lighter structures, hydrogen fuel and refuelling, in [Braslow 1999][book_braslow_1999].

The operational phase never took place. The modification was never made, and the reason recorded by the programme's NASA consultant, in [Braslow 1999][book_braslow_1999], is the resource demands of the war in Vietnam, not any finding about laminar flow.

### The Maintenance the Programme Did Record

**The programme was not silent on maintenance, and what it recorded points away from insects.** In August 1963 [Butz 1963][ref_butz_1963] reported that the early flight results had substantiated an estimate that the airframe of a laminar flow control aircraft would cost about ten percent more to maintain than a conventional one, that the wing would probably not need cleaning after every flight, and that steam cleaning once a month would keep it adequately free of dirt and grease. He also reported that insects sticking to the wing had not so far bothered the suction system. The review group confirmed that insect strikes had not disturbed the laminar flow, and warned in the same sentence that the flights had been made over a desert where insects are sparse and small, so the result said nothing about operation worldwide, in [Braslow 1999][book_braslow_1999]. [Joslin 1998b][ref_joslin_1998], summarising Fowell and Antonatos, shows that the ground maintenance time went mostly to repairing the epoxy-filled splices between wing panels. **The maintenance burden the X-21 did record was a manufacturing defect, not an operational one**, and the review group's new metal upper surfaces were meant to remove it.

## Comparison With Ground Prediction

The wind tunnel and glove work of the 1950s predicted that suction would hold laminar flow to high chord Reynolds numbers, and the flight article confirmed it. On that axis the ground prediction was good. [Joslin 1998b][ref_joslin_1998] records about 160 hours of high-speed and 1,300 hours of low-speed wind tunnel testing of a model X-21A wing, and good agreement between tunnel and flight once aeroelastic deformation and the flight pressure distributions were allowed for. The suction requirement was the exception, measured 50 percent above prediction at Mach 0.74 and 41,400 feet, so the theory erred on the side that costs power. Companion tunnel tests showed that a sudden loss of laminar flow would not endanger the aircraft, although the lateral-directional and long-period longitudinal motions might need more artificial damping than a turbulent aircraft.

**On two axes it was not, and both failures were of the same kind.** The severity of spanwise contamination had been seen in British swept-wing experiments and in small-scale tunnel tests, and its significance had gone unrecognised until a full-size swept wing met it. The surface tolerance achievable in manufacture had likewise been explored only on small-scale structural samples rather than on wings, and a wing has splices where a sample does not, in [Braslow 1999][book_braslow_1999].

**Neither is a failure of theory.** Both are failures of scale, in the specific sense that the feature which caused the trouble did not exist on the model. A tunnel model has no spanwise splice and no fuselage junction of the relevant size. The transition-prediction methods that have since been built to carry this kind of analysis are [L. Cutrone et al, Transition Prediction in Hyperson][research_l_cutrone], [Meelan Choudhari et al][research_meelan_choudhari], [Mack 1984][research_leslie_m_mack_1984], [Bushnell et al. 1988][research_bushnell_1988], [Hall and Smith 1988][research_hall_smith_1988], [Hall and Smith 1989][research_hall_smith_1989], [Iyer and Spall 1991][research_iyer_spall_1991], [Su 2015][research_su_2015], [Zhou et al. 2015][research_zhou_2015], [Kosarev et al. 2016][research_kosarev_2016], [Xia and Chen 2016][research_xia_chen_2016], [Mishriky and Walsh 2018][research_mishriky_walsh_2018], [Zhou et al. 2018][research_zhou_2018], [Han and Cao 2019][research_han_cao_2019], [Introduction to the Special 2019][research_introduction_to_2019], [Martín and Paredes 2021][research_martin_paredes_2021], [Nie et al. 2022][research_nie_2022], [Niu and Su 2023][research_niu_su_2023], [Hildebrand et al. 2025][research_nathaniel_hildebrand_2025], [Yan et al. 2025b][research_yan_2025].

## What the Data Changed

**Laminar flow control stopped being a question of physics and became a question of engineering practice**, and the change is due more to this programme than to any other.

Attachment-line contamination went from a problem whose significance had gone unrecognised to a named design constraint with a criterion attached, one that requires careful attention in the design of any large laminar wing. The manufacturing tolerance problem was shown to be solvable, expensively. The atmospheric particle problem was identified and left open for want of data, in [Braslow 1999][book_braslow_1999].

**What did not change is the thing the programme is remembered for.** No service-maintenance evaluation was made, and the maintenance it did log was dominated by repairs to filled splices, so nothing was learned about whether a well-made wing could be kept clean in service, and the widespread belief that the X-21 settled that question is not supported by the record of the programme.

## The Contemporary Literature

| Cluster | Records |
|---|---|
| Suction laminar flow control and its flight research | 166 |
| Hybrid laminar flow control | 70 |
| Natural laminar flow and low-drag aerofoils | 200 |
| The attachment line and leading-edge contamination | 86 |
| Crossflow instability on swept wings and its control | 223 |
| Roughness, waviness and surface tolerance | 112 |
| Turbulent spots and wedges | 19 |
| Boundary-layer stability and transition | 260 |
| Ice crystals, cloud and the icing of aircraft | 39 |
| Insect contamination and its mitigation | 18 |
| Coatings against ice and erosion | 32 |
| Suction systems, perforated surfaces and their manufacture | 45 |
| Suction and injection in the boundary layer | 102 |
| Skin friction and the turbulent boundary layer | 49 |
| Riblets and the reduction of turbulent skin friction | 114 |
| Induced drag, span efficiency, cruise and aircraft design | 25 |
| **Total** | **1,560** |

Laminar flow control did not die in 1965. It went quiet for a decade, revived when fuel prices made drag expensive, and has been a continuous field ever since, as the clusters below show, and the revival and its later reviews are recorded in [Povinelli et al. 1976][research_povinelli_1976], [Braslow and Muraca 1978][research_braslow_muraca_1978], [Klineberg 1978][research_klineberg_1978], [Aircraft Energy Efficiency Program 1979][research_aircraft_energy_1979], [Wagner et al. 1988b][research_wagner_bartlett_1988], [Joslin 1998b][research_joslin_1998_b], [Saric and Reed 2004][research_saric_reed_2004], [Green 2008][research_green_2008], [Atkin 2008][research_atkin_2008], [Beck et al. 2018][research_beck_2018].

**But it is not the same technology.** Every major line of work since the X-21 has conceded something the X-21 did not, and each concession buys the same thing, a surface that can be built to tolerance and kept clean in service. **The field retreated from the full-chord slotted wing, and it spent the sixty years since on the question the X-21 was cancelled before it could ask.**

### The First Concession Gives Up Aft Suction

Hybrid laminar flow control applies suction only near the leading edge and lets a favourable pressure gradient carry the flow from there, in [Hybrid Laminar Flow Control 1982][research_hybrid_laminar_1982], [Collier et al. 1992][research_collier_1992], [Wie et al. 1992][research_wie_1992], [High Reynolds Number Hybrid 1999a, High Reynolds Number Hybrid Lamin][research_high_reynolds_1999], [Young and Fielding 2001b][research_young_fielding_2001], [Young et al. 2001][research_young_2001], [Young et al. 2003][research_young_2003], [Eppink and Wlezien 2011][research_jenna_l_eppink_richard_wlezien_2011], [Risse and Stumpf 2014][research_risse_stumpf_2014], [Vermeersch and Bouteiller 2014][research_vermeersch_bouteiller_2014], [Shahneh 2016][research_shahneh_2016], [Krishnan et al. 2017][research_krishnan_2017], [Kalarikovilagam Srinivasan and Bertram 2019][research_kalarikovilagam_srinivasan_bertram_2019], [Yang et al. 2019][research_yang_2019], [Shi et al. 2020][research_shi_2020], [Lüdeke and Breitenstein 2021][research_ludeke_breitenstein_2021], [Schrauf and von Geyr 2021a][research_schrauf_von_geyr_2021], [Sudhi et al. 2021][research_sudhi_2021], [Barklage et al. 2022][research_barklage_2022], [Jentys et al. 2022][research_jentys_2022], [Yang et al. 2022c][research_yang_2022], [Zare Shahnehb 2022][research_zare_shahnehb_2022], [Jentys and Breitsamter 2023][research_jentys_breitsamter_2023], [Lobitz et al. 2023][research_lobitz_2023], [Shi et al. 2023][research_shi_2023], [Sudhi et al. 2023][research_sudhi_2023], [Karpuk and Mosca 2024][research_karpuk_mosca_2024], [Athar et al. 2025][research_athar_2025], [Fröhler et al. 2025][research_frohler_2025], [Brinza et al. 2026][research_brinza_2026], [Corelli Grappadelli et al. 2026][research_corelli_grappadelli_2026], [Wong et al. 2026][research_wong_2026], [Zhang et al. 2026][research_zhang_2026].

**That is a direct response to the X-21's cost problem.** The leading edge is a small fraction of the chord, so the plumbing shrinks greatly, the structural intrusion largely disappears, and the wing behind the front spar becomes an ordinary wing. What is given up is the aft laminar run, and with it a meaningful part of the drag saving. **The trade is worse aerodynamically and much better industrially**, and every subsequent decision in this field has had that shape.

### The Second Concession Gives Up Suction Altogether

Natural laminar flow shapes the aerofoil so that the pressure gradient alone holds the boundary layer laminar, asking nothing of the plumbing because there is none. The cluster on natural laminar flow and low-drag aerofoils below holds its literature, and its flight record includes [Montoya et al. 1981][research_montoya_1981], [Wentz et al. 1984][research_wentz_1984], [Runyan et al. 1987][research_runyan_bielak_1987], [Horstmann et al. 1990b][research_horstmann_redeker_1990], [Frederick et al. 2014][research_frederick_2014], [Tokugawa et al. 2026][research_tokugawa_2026].

**The constraint moves from the wing structure to the wing shape**, and the shape constraint is severe. A natural laminar flow wing cannot be swept much, because sweep drives the crossflow instability that no pressure gradient can suppress, and the X-21's thirty degrees is already beyond what natural laminar flow tolerates. That confines it to slower and smaller aircraft.

### The Insect Question the X-21 Never Reached

The X-21's service-maintenance objective was never reached. **The literature took it up in the late 1970s and has worked on it since**, in [Fisher and Peterson 1978][research_fisher_peterson_1978], [Peterson and Fisher 1978][research_peterson_fisher_1978], [Maresh and Bragg 1984][research_maresh_bragg_1984], [Croom and Holmes 1985][research_croom_holmes_1985], [Croom and Holmes 1986][research_croom_holmes_1986], [Lorenzi et al. 2011a][research_lorenzi_wohl_2011], [Kok et al. 2013][research_kok_mertens_2013], [Siochi et al. 2013][research_siochi_smith_2013], [Wohl et al. 2013][research_wohl_smith_2013], [Kok and Young 2014][research_kok_young_2014], [Kok et al. 2014][research_kok_tobin_2014], [Kok et al. 2015][research_kok_2015], [Shanahan et al. 2015][research_shanahan_wohl_2015], [Wohl et al. 2015][research_wohl_doss_2015], [Alexander et al. 2016][research_alexander_harris_2016], [Krishnan et al. 2016][research_krishnan_robison_2016], [Wicke et al. 2016][research_wicke_linke_2016], [Kok et al. 2017][research_kok_tobin_2017].

**The modern answer is chemistry and geometry rather than cleaning.** Coatings that insect residue cannot adhere to, surfaces engineered at the micrometre scale so that a strike does not leave a critical roughness, and shields deployed over the leading edge during the low-altitude part of the flight where the insects are. Covering the leading edge is not new, since a paper cover protected the F-94 glove from insects in the 1950s, in [Joslin 1998b][ref_joslin_1998], so the last of these is a mechanical answer the X-21 could have carried.

**Here the contemporary literature bears most sharply on this programme.** The problem widely cited as having killed laminar flow control is the one problem the programme never evaluated, and it has turned out to be tractable, in part by coatings and engineered surfaces developed since.

### Ice Crystals Remain Unsettled

The X-21 lost laminar flow in cirrus and recovered it on leaving. The article computes why both happen within milliseconds. **What it could not compute in 1965, and what is still not fully answered, is how much of a real route is spent in such cloud**, in [Pueschel et al. 1996][research_pueschel_1996], [Jensen et al. 2008][research_jensen_2008], [Oliver 2015][research_oliver_2015], [Leroy et al. 2016][research_leroy_2016], [Leroy et al. 2017][research_leroy_2017], [Sullivan et al. 2017][research_sullivan_2017], [Norde et al. 2018][research_norde_2018], [Bravin and Strapp 2019][research_bravin_strapp_2019], [Gurevich et al. 2019][research_gurevich_2019], [Haggerty et al. 2019][research_haggerty_2019], [Tan and Lai 2019][research_tan_lai_2019], [Xiao et al. 2019][research_xiao_2019], [Flegel 2021][research_flegel_2021], [Hu et al. 2021][research_hu_2021], [Rugg et al. 2021][research_rugg_2021], [Hu et al. 2022][research_hu_2022], [Yang et al. 2022a][research_yang_2022_2], [Potts et al. 2023][research_potts_2023], [Yang et al. 2023][research_yang_2023_2], [Malik et al. 2024][research_malik_2024], [Peters et al. 2024][research_peters_2024], [Tatsuta et al. 2024][research_tatsuta_2024], [Ma et al. 2025a][research_ma_2025_2], [Yang et al. 2026][research_yang_2026].

The modern literature approaches this from a different direction, because high ice water content became an engine problem before it was ever resolved as a laminar flow problem. **The measurements now exist because ice crystals were damaging turbofans**, an odd route to the data the X-21 needed, and the coverage is still organised around convective cloud at tropical latitudes rather than around cruise-altitude cirrus on transport routes, in [Bravin and Strapp 2019][research_bravin_strapp_2019] and [Leroy et al. 2017][research_leroy_2017].

### The Crossflow Is Now Controlled Rather Than Suffered

The instability that made a swept laminar wing hard is no longer merely endured. The cluster on crossflow instability below collects its literature, and the control by roughness elements and plasma actuators described next is in [Saric 1997][research_saric_1997], [Carpenter et al. 2009][research_carpenter_saric_2009], [Malik et al. 2013][research_malik_liao_2013], [Saric et al. 2015][research_saric_west_2015], [Nicholson et al. 2019][research_nicholson_duan_2019], [Duchmann et al. 2014][research_duchmann_2014], [Dörr and Kloker 2015][research_dorr_kloker_2015], [Wang et al. 2018][research_wang_2018], [Yadala et al. 2018][research_yadala_2018].

**The elegant result is that roughness can be used against itself.** Discrete roughness elements placed at a chosen spacing near the leading edge excite a crossflow mode that is not the most amplified one, and that mode then suppresses the dangerous one through nonlinear interaction. **A wing is made more laminar by being made rougher in a specific way**, which would have been an unwelcome sentence in a programme spending two years chasing thousandths of an inch of waviness. Plasma actuators do the same job with no surface change at all.

### Transition Prediction Since the X-21

The X-21 was designed with stability criteria and correlations and flown with trailing-edge total-pressure rakes, in [Joslin 1998b][ref_joslin_1998].
