The design tool that would have let Northrop compute the growth of disturbances through the full three-dimensional boundary layer did not exist, and the tools that replaced it are [Petzold and Radespiel 2015][research_petzold_radespiel_2015], [Pérez et al. 2015][research_perez_2015], [Roberts et al. 2015][research_roberts_2015], [Wedin et al. 2015][research_wedin_2015], [Wedin and Cherubini 2016][research_wedin_cherubini_2016], [Евстигнеев and Рябков 2016][research___2016], [Egorov et al. 2017][research_egorov_2017], [Egorov et al. 2018][research_egorov_2018], [Grigor'ev and Ershov 2019][research_grigorev_ershov_2019], [He et al. 2019][research_he_2019], [Kudryavtsev and Khotyanovsky 2019][research_kudryavtsev_khotyanovsky_2019], [Hildebrand et al. 2020][research_hildebrand_2020], [Morgan and Davies 2020][research_morgan_davies_2020], [Yalcin et al. 2021][research_yalcin_2021], [Toppings and Yarusevych 2023][research_toppings_yarusevych_2023], [Chaturvedi et al. 2024][research_chaturvedi_2024], [Zou and Zhong 2024][research_zou_zhong_2024].

**This article's attachment-line calculation is the first term of what those methods now solve properly.** A criterion with a threshold has become a stability computation over the full three-dimensional boundary layer, and the crossflow and attachment-line modes that the X-21 met one at a time and by surprise now appear together in a single analysis before anything is built.

### Making the Surface Is Still the Hard Part

Sixty years on, the manufacturing problem the programme spent its first two years on remains the one that decides whether the technology is affordable, in [Drake et al. 2005][research_drake_2005], [Bender and Drake 2006][research_bender_drake_2006], [Li et al. 2024b][research_li_2024_3], [Dou et al. 2026][research_dou_2026], [Kashaev et al. 2026][research_kashaev_2026].

**What has changed is the aperture.** The X-21 cut slots and drilled more than eight hundred thousand metering holes with purpose-built tooling, in [Butz 1963][ref_butz_1963]. The modern surface is laser-drilled microperforated titanium, with holes measured in tens of micrometres and counted in millions, in [Joslin 1998b][ref_joslin_1998]. **The tolerance requirement has not relaxed at all**, and by the roughness criterion in this article it never could have, since it is set by the physics of the boundary layer and not by the state of the art.

### Flight Demonstrations Since

**What followed was a sequence of smaller and more targeted experiments**, in [Montoya et al. 1981][research_montoya_1981], [Fischer et al. 1983][research_fischer_wright_1983], [Wagner et al. 1986][research_wagner_1986], [Hastings et al. 1987][research_hastings_1987], [Fisher and Fischer 1987][research_fisher_fischer_1987], [Runyan et al. 1987][research_runyan_bielak_1987], [Wagner et al. 1988a][research_wagner_1988], [Wagner et al. 1989][research_wagner_1989], [Horstmann et al. 1990b][research_horstmann_redeker_1990], [Wagner et al. 1992][research_wagner_1992], [Collier 1993][research_collier_1993], [Barry et al. 1994][research_barry_parke_1994], [Anders and Fischer 1999][research_anders_fischer_1999], [High Reynolds Number Hybrid 1999a][research_high_reynolds_1999], [Saric et al. 2015][research_saric_west_2015], [Shanahan et al. 2015][research_shanahan_wohl_2015], [Tucker et al. 2016a][research_tucker_2016], [Yang et al. 2022b][research_yang_2022_4].

Gloves on transport wings and fins, and a supersonic laminar flow experiment on a delta, in [Joslin 1998b][ref_joslin_1998]. **Every one of them is narrower than the X-21.**

### The Installed Cost of Suction

The article charges the suction system a thermodynamic power and states that it does not charge weight, volume or structural penalty. The titles in this survey that name the suction system or its power are [Maddalon and Braslow 1990][research_maddalon_braslow_1990], [High Reynolds Number Hybrid 1999b][research_high_reynolds_1999_2], [Yasuaki and Egami 1999][research_yasuaki_egami_1999], [Saeed et al. 2010][research_saeed_graham_2010], [Saeed et al. 2011][research_saeed_2011], [Perry and Ansell 2017][research_perry_ansell_2017], [Baranov et al. 2021][research_baranov_kiselev_2021], [Prasannakumar et al. 2024][research_prasannakumar_2024], and they are few.

**That thinness is informative.** The installed cost of a suction system is the quantity a manufacturer needs and also the one hardest to publish, because it is specific to an airframe and commercially sensitive. The public literature is rich on the aerodynamics and poor on the installation, the same imbalance the X-21 programme had.

### Riblets as the Competing Route

Riblets and their successors do not prevent turbulence. They reduce the friction of turbulence that is already there. The riblet cluster below gathers that literature, and representative records are [Walsh 1982][research_walsh_1982], [Walsh and Lindemann 1984][research_walsh_lindemann_1984], [Lazos and Wilkinson 1988][research_lazos_wilkinson_1988], [Bixler and Bhushan 2013][research_bixler_bhushan_2013], [Benschop and Breugem 2017][research_benschop_breugem_2017], [Catalano et al. 2020][research_catalano_2020], [Ricco et al. 2021][research_ricco_2021], [Gatti et al. 2025][research_gatti_2025].

**They give a few percent where laminar flow gives tens of percent, and they ask almost nothing of the aircraft.** No plumbing, no power, no structural intrusion, and a surface that is a film rather than a wing. That ratio explains why they keep returning, and it states most clearly the trade inside which this subject sits. **The prize scales with how much of the boundary layer you control, and so does the cost of controlling it.**

### What the Survey Shows

**The X-21 was right about the physics and the field has never disputed it.** Laminar flow control works, the suction power costs less than the drag it saves, and nothing in sixty years has overturned either statement.

**What the field did instead was retreat from the installation.** Hybrid laminar flow control gave up the aft suction. Natural laminar flow gave up the suction. Riblets gave up laminar flow. Each step traded aerodynamic benefit for a surface that could be manufactured and maintained, and every one of those trades is a decision about the question the X-21 never got to answer.

**And that question is now being answered in the affirmative.** Insect residue is being attacked by engineered surfaces and coatings rather than by cleaning, the crossflow is being controlled instead of avoided, and the surface is being made by processes unavailable to the programme. The verdict that came to be passed on this programme was that laminar flow control was impractical to maintain. **It was passed without evidence, and the evidence that has accumulated since largely contradicts it.**

### Suction laminar flow control and its flight research

**The keystone.** Laminar flow control by suction as a programme, from the Northrop and NACA suction work of the 1940s and 1950s through the X-21 to the leading-edge flight test on NASA's JetStar, the Langley 8-foot tunnel experiment, the F-16XL supersonic experiment and the transport design studies of the energy efficiency programme. 19 of its 166 titles name flight.

### Ice crystals, cloud and the icing of aircraft

**The atmospheric problem.** Ice crystals and cloud particles, their measurement and their effect on laminar flow, with the icing of aircraft surfaces kept as a neighbouring subject of the same leading edge. 7 of the 39 titles name laminar flow, and 12 name an engine or high ice water content, which is the route by which most of the modern measurement arrived.

## Where the Framing Breaks Down

The calculation above treats the suction system as a compressor restoring a total pressure, and charges the aircraft the ideal work divided by an efficiency. **That is a thermodynamic accounting, not an installation.** It does not include the weight of the ducts and turbines, the volume they take from the fuel tanks, the drag of the nacelles that house them, or the structural penalty of a skin perforated by some two hundred slots. A real trade would carry all of those, and they act against the technology, not for it.

The whole-aircraft figures rest on an assumed wetted-area breakdown, an assumed form factor and an assumed non-friction drag increment. They are the weakest numbers in the article and are offered as an order of magnitude. **They are also the numbers most sensitive to consistent bookkeeping.** A zero-lift drag coefficient that implies a form factor, set against a laminarised area credited with a flat-plate saving that carries none, understates the benefit by about a quarter, so the coefficient is built here from its parts and the charge and the credit use the same basis.

The aspect-ratio argument inverts a drag polar that already contains three assumptions, so the figure of 11.8 should be read as evidence that the required planform is very different rather than as a specification for it.

The attachment-line calculation assumes a leading-edge radius, a taper ratio and that the quoted sweep is measured at the quarter chord. The conclusion drawn from it, that the wing root sits near the contamination threshold, survives across the plausible range, but the precise value does not.

**The suction coefficient is the least well constrained input of all.** The value used is an assumed design figure rather than a measured X-21 quantity, and the entire margin over break-even scales inversely with it. The local values the programme reports are two and a half to five times higher, and the reported flow rates can be read either way, so the margin of 5.8 is an upper estimate for the nominal machinery and the true figure for the X-21 lies somewhere between about one and about four.

The cloud calculation assumes a number density of ten thousand crystals per cubic metre, which no source states. Hall's typical cirrus gives nearly nine times as many, so the assumption errs toward understating the effect, and the threshold of about eight crystals per cubic metre for erratic operation inherits every crudeness of the wedge model, including the assumption that every wedge starts at the leading edge and spans the mean chord.

## The Source Base

The programme is unusually well documented for one that ended without a production application, and the reason is that a NASA engineer who consulted on it wrote its history thirty years later.

[Braslow 1999][book_braslow_1999] is the load-bearing source for the programme narrative, including the Vietnam attribution, and it reproduces the primary document behind the rest, the report of the review group that met in October 1965. [Kosin 1965][research_kosin_1965] is the contemporary engineering description in the Journal of Aircraft, and [Wheldon and Whites 1966][research_wheldon_whites_1966] is a paper on the flight testing of the aeroplane. The most useful description of the wing's internal construction is [Butz 1963][ref_butz_1963], a trade-press account published while the aeroplane was flying, which is where the distinction between the slots and the metering holes is stated plainly. [Hall 1964][ref_hall_1964] is the Northrop report on the cloud problem, now on the NASA Technical Reports Server. The quantitative results of the Northrop final report, its suction coefficients, flow rates, tolerances and year-by-year laminar extents, are known here through the summary in [Joslin 1998b][ref_joslin_1998], because the Northrop reports themselves, held by the Defense Technical Information Center, were not retrievable. Where this article quotes those numbers it quotes a careful secondary summary of a primary document rather than the document.

**A caution about the secondary literature.** The figure of eight hundred thousand slots is repeated by reference works, encyclopaedias and aviation histories alike. The 1963 description in [Butz 1963][ref_butz_1963] makes clear that the number counts drilled holes connecting plenums to ducts, and the slot spacings Joslin reports put the slots at of the order of a hundred on each surface. **The error is not consequential for the physics and it is very consequential for the impression the reader forms**, since it converts a precisely engineered surface into something that sounds like a colander.

**The research survey holds 1,560 research records drawn from the NASA Technical Reports Server, the Defense Technical Information Center, the Office of Scientific and Technical Information and the journal literature indexed by Crossref**, of which 456 are report primaries, being 29.2 percent, by the identifier rule set out under The Definition of Primary. **The median publication year is 2000.** Of the 1,539 records that carry a year, 186, or 12.1 percent, are dated 1965 or earlier, the period in which the aircraft was designed and flown, and 640, or 41.6 percent, are dated 2011 or later, so the reference base leans toward the contemporary literature.

### The Definition of Primary

**Primacy is a property of the document, and this survey derives it from the identifier.** A record is a report primary when its address is on the NASA Technical Reports Server or the Office of Scientific and Technical Information, or when it carries a digital object identifier under the 10.21236 prefix, which Crossref registers for Defense Technical Information Center reports. **The Northrop summaries of laminar boundary layer control research and the reports of NASA's leading-edge flight test are report primaries under that rule**, and so is Braslow and Knox's simplified method for the critical roughness height. **The rule does not reach first-hand papers published in journals**, such as [Kosin 1965][research_kosin_1965] on the suction system of the X-21A and [Schubauer and Skramstad 1947][research_schubauer_skramstad_1947] on laminar boundary-layer oscillations, so 29.2 percent is a lower bound on how much of the survey is first-hand.

### Thin Subjects

**Six subjects that this article's derivations depend on have thin literatures.** Each count below is the number of research records in this survey whose title carries at least one of the listed terms, with a hyphen read as a space. Where a row joins two groups of terms with the word together, a title must carry a term from each group.

| Subject | Title terms counted | Records |
|---|---|---|
| the X-21 programme itself | X-21, X-21A, X 21, X 21A | 2 |
| maintenance and service experience of a laminar wing | maintenance, maintainability, airline service, operational experience, operational assessment, operational considerations, durability, in service | 6 |
| cruise performance and the range of a transport | cruise, long range, best range, global range, Breguet | 14 |
| ice particles acting on laminar flow | ice, ice crystal, ice crystals, cloud, clouds, particle, particles, cloud encounter, cloud encounters together with laminar, laminar flow, LFC | 11 |
| turbulent spots and wedges | turbulent spot, turbulent spots, turbulent wedge, turbulent wedges | 19 |
| the power, weight and installation of the suction system | suction power, suction system, suction systems, pumping power, power requirement, power requirements, suction pump, suction pumps | 8 |

**The aeroplane has almost no literature of its own.** Two titles name the X-21, [Kosin 1965][research_kosin_1965] on its suction system and [Wheldon and Whites 1966][research_wheldon_whites_1966] on its flight testing, so the programme's record rests on Braslow's history and Joslin's summary rather than on a body of papers.

**The question the programme never answered has six titles.** The earliest is from 1986, two decades after the review group asked for an operational evaluation, and three of them report simulated airline service of laminar flow control leading-edge systems.

**The particle problem is mostly one decade's work.** Of the eleven titles that join ice, cloud or particles to laminar flow, seven date from 1982 to 1989, the decade of NASA's leading-edge flight test, and three of the rest concern ice accretion or ice protection rather than particles in the boundary layer.

**The wedge model rests on the spot literature.** One of the 19 titles on spots and wedges names a turbulent wedge, so the spreading angle the cloud calculation uses comes from studies of the spot.

**The installed suction system is named in eight titles and its power in one.** That is the quantity a manufacturer needs and the one this article does not compute.

### What the Survey Admits

**A record is admitted only when a person reading its title finds it on this article's subject**, the subject being suction laminar flow control, the stability theory behind it, the surfaces it needs and the insects and ice that contaminate them. **The phrase boundary layer belongs to meteorology as much as to aerodynamics**, and a whole journal is devoted to the atmospheric boundary layer. Operating-room laminar air flow, crossflow filtration and a Cretaceous stick-insect fossil share this subject's vocabulary in the same way and none of them is part of it.

**One homonym in this subject lives inside aerodynamics rather than outside it.** Boundary layer control means suction to hold laminar flow and reduce drag, and it also means suction or blowing to delay separation and increase lift. They share a name, a mechanism and often an author, and they are different technologies answering different questions, so a title that names boundary layer control does not by itself place a record in this subject. Records that control separation or stall by plasma, jets, fluidic oscillators, tubercles or flexible surfaces, without suction through the wall, are excluded on that ground.

Many words this subject depends on also name something else, and a record that shares only such a word is excluded. Perforated also names panels made to absorb sound, laminar also names flames and the internal flow and heat transfer of tubes, channels and fins, and coating also names protection against corrosion and the surfacing of pavements, insulators and face masks. Non-Newtonian, magnetohydrodynamic and porous-medium flows, chemical mixers, fuel cells, surgery, seaweed and power grids share the vocabulary of the boundary layer without its subject. Transition also names the conversion of a tilting aircraft from hover to forward flight, noise also names the sound an aerofoil radiates, and attachment also names separated flows reattaching behind a step or a ramp. Bluff bodies and their wakes, turbomachinery blades and cascades, the chemistry of re-entry boundary layers and the interaction of shock waves with separated flow are excluded for the same reason. Notices of erratum, correction, retraction or withdrawal, figure, table and supplementary-file records, peer-review reports and journal front matter are excluded as well, because a survey counts research works and those are parts of works or editorial events rather than works, and a second deposit of one document is counted once. A review of a book, a dictionary or short encyclopedia entry and a notice such as an editorial, an announcement or a news item are not research works either and are excluded.

**Some records of doubtful relevance are kept.** Laminar flow with suction or injection through the porous wall of a tube or channel, magnetohydrodynamic boundary-layer control with suction, suction for high lift, anti-icing and leading-edge erosion coatings including those for wind turbine blades, the icing of aircraft surfaces, and drag reduction by polymers and riblets in liquids each share with this subject either the mechanism of wall suction, the problem of a contaminated leading edge or the aim of reducing skin friction, so each is kept and a reader may weigh it accordingly. Transition at supersonic and hypersonic speeds is kept because it is the same stability theory.

**Every research title has been read for relevance, so the off-topic share that remains is a matter of reading judgement.** A reading of titles cannot show that a work is relevant in substance, so this is a statement about titles only.

## Epistemic State

**Established by the programme and not in dispute.** Laminar flow was obtained and held on a large swept wing over more than ninety-five percent of the intended area. Surface tolerance and spanwise contamination were the two problems that consumed the schedule, and both were overcome, in [Braslow 1999][book_braslow_1999].

**Computed here, and dependent on stated assumptions.** The suction penalty is about a sixth of the friction saving at the nominal cruise condition, the break-even suction coefficient is about six times the design value, and the suction drag coefficient is independent of altitude. The last of those is exact given the model. The first two carry the uncertainty of the suction coefficient and the duct recovery. Against the local suction coefficients of the Northrop final report, the margin falls to between about one and two, so what survives the primary record is that suction cost less than it saved, not that it cost a sixth.

**Inferred, and offered as inference.** That the X-21 as a converted bomber could not have demonstrated more than about twelve percent in range follows from the wetted-area accounting, and the wetted areas are estimated. That the 1963 claim of a lift-to-drag ratio above thirty is unreachable on this planform at any level of laminarisation follows from induced drag alone consuming 73 percent of the drag budget such a ratio allows, and that argument is stronger than the wetted-area one because it does not depend on how much of the aeroplane is laminarised.

**Asserted widely and not supported by the record.** That the X-21 demonstrated laminar flow control to be impractical on maintenance grounds. The maintenance evaluation was not performed. The maintenance the programme did record went mostly to repairing filled splices, and insects did not disturb the laminar flow in the desert where it flew, in [Joslin 1998b][ref_joslin_1998] and [Braslow 1999][book_braslow_1999].

**Read in the record, with a caveat on wording.** [Braslow 1999][book_braslow_1999] writes that the service-experience objective had not even been initiated by October 1965. The review group's report says the programme had not been able completely to address the operational questions. Both agree that no operational evaluation took place.

**Unknown then and largely unknown still.** The operational significance of atmospheric ice particles, for want of statistics on particle size and concentration along real routes, in [Braslow 1999][book_braslow_1999].

## Out of Scope

Supersonic laminar flow control, which the programme discussed and did not test, and which later became its own subject. The detailed stability theory of the slotted surface, including the disturbances the slots themselves introduce. The structural and weight penalties of the suction wing, which are named as a limitation above and not computed. The economics of laminar flow control for commercial operators, which belongs to the transport design studies, not to this aeroplane.

## Conclusion

The X-21A did the hard part. It held laminar flow on a large swept wing in real air, having first beaten a surface-tolerance problem and an attachment-line problem that nobody had properly anticipated, and it did so with a suction system that, on the assumptions used here, cost about a sixth of what it returned, and on the local coefficients the programme reports cost between about two fifths and most of it, since the penalty fraction of 0.172 scales with the coefficient to 0.43 at $5 \times 10^{-4}$ and 0.86 at $10 \times 10^{-4}$.

**The power trade was never the difficulty.** The margin over break-even is a factor of six at the assumed suction coefficient and nearer two at the reported local values, the penalty falls with Mach number, and it does not depend on altitude at all. An aeroplane designed around this from the start would have collected far more of it than a converted bomber ever could, and the case for the technology was accordingly always made about an aircraft that did not exist.

What the programme was for, in the end, was to find out whether such a wing could be lived with. **It never found out.** The maintenance objective had not been reached when the funding stopped, the recommended wing modification that would have made the evaluation meaningful was never made, and the aeroplane was retired to Edwards to be photographed, in the [Northrop X-21A][ref_x21] reference entry. The verdict that laminar flow control is impractical because of maintenance was reached anyway.

**That makes this aircraft unusual in a series full of failures.** The [X-19][related_post_a316_curtiss_wright_x19] fell out of the sky. The [X-20][related_post_a317_boeing_x20] was cancelled without flying. The X-21 succeeded at what it was asked to do and was then remembered for failing at something it was never allowed to attempt.
